I used to feel like I was someone people could count on, and now I get the sense they’re not so sure anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're fidgeting or have an urge to move around when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, especially if I’m supposed to be sitting still for a while.\n\nLike at church meetings, I’ll catch myself tapping my foot, or rubbing the edge of my purse over and over, or shifting around in the chair. And then I notice the ladies beside me looking over, and I think, “Oh, stop doing that,” but a minute later I’m doing something else with my hands.\n\nIt’s funny, because those chairs aren’t very comfortable anyway, and last month they had the room too warm, and I kept thinking someone should crack a window… but yes, I guess the answer is yes. I do have a hard time just sitting still.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things even when there's no obvious reason to worry?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… worry, yes, but I don’t know if it’s for no reason. There’s usually something, even if it’s small, like wondering if I forgot to shut something off or if I said the wrong thing to somebody.\n\nBut then again, sometimes I’ll be sitting there and my mind just grabs onto something. Like the other day I was looking at the mail on the table, and then I remembered I needed stamps, and then I started thinking about the post office parking lot because it’s always so crowded there… so I don’t know if that counts as worrying exactly.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’m just worried all the time for no reason. I’d have to think about it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these feelings of restlessness and difficulty focusing?\n\nPatient: Well… it really started causing real trouble after my husband died. That was about… a year and ten months ago now, I think.\n\nI mean, I suppose I’ve had little things before, everyone does, but since then it’s been much harder to keep myself settled and on track. Time gets funny after someone passes, you know—people say “almost two years” and it feels like yesterday and forever at the same time. But yes, the real problems with it have been since then.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to think about something else?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure exactly what you mean by repeating thoughts. I mean, I’ll get a thought stuck for a bit, like remembering I need to call someone or wondering where I put something, but I don’t know if it’s the kind of thing you’re asking about.\n\nSometimes a song gets in my head—oh, that happens at church too, especially if the choir sings one of the old hymns my mother liked, and then I’ll be humming it later without realizing. But thoughts… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed them repeating in a way that feels strange.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself frequently worrying about ordinary, everyday things, like finances, health, or relationships?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say frequently. I mean, there are ordinary things I think about, of course—everybody has bills come in, or a doctor’s appointment on the calendar, or someone in the family you haven’t heard from in a while.\n\nBut I’m not sure it’s worry like you mean. Sometimes I’ll start thinking about one thing and then it turns into something else, like I’ll remember the electric bill and then think about the kitchen light, and then that reminds me I still haven’t cleaned out that one drawer with all the old batteries in it… and then I’ve lost what I was doing.\n\nSo, everyday things, yes, they come up. But I don’t know that I sit and worry over finances or health or relationships all the time. I’d have to pay more attention to it, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worrying about everyday things is more than what most people would worry about?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to measure that, honestly. I don’t sit there thinking, “Oh, I worry more than other people.” I mean, my neighbor worries about everything—she’ll tell you the whole story about her gutters if you let her—and then my sister hardly seems to worry at all, or at least she doesn’t say it out loud.\n\nFor me, it’s more that things come into my head and I follow them around a bit. Whether that’s more than normal… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worries about everyday things are causing you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Significant distress… I don’t know. I wouldn’t put it that strongly, maybe. I mean, some things bother me, sure, but then I get busy with something else, or I remember I was supposed to take something out of the freezer—actually I did that yesterday and then left it on the counter too long, which was annoying.\n\nBut distress from worrying? I’m not sure. I haven’t really thought of it that way. Things can get in my head, but I don’t know if I’d say it upsets me a lot.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Oh, no, that goes way back. It’s not just something that started recently. Things can get in my head, but I don’t know if I’d say it upsets me a lot.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant problems with restlessness or difficulty focusing since childhood, or have these issues developed more recently in your life?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, that goes way back. It’s not just something that started recently. Which, honestly, sounds about right. I’d start something and then get distracted by someone sharpening a pencil or a bird outside the window or whatever. Yes, it’s not just one place. At home it shows up a lot with bills and forms and that kind of paperwork—my family’s had to step in more than once because I either put it somewhere “safe” and then can’t find it, or I don’t finish it.

Like checking things, or doing the same thing a set number of times?\n\nI don’t think I have anything like that, not that I’ve really noticed. I mean, I’ll go back into a room and forget why I went there, and then end up doing something else entirely—yesterday I found myself standing by the sink with a towel in my hand, and I had started out looking for my glasses—but I don’t think that’s what you mean by repeating. So… no, not really, unless I’m misunderstanding you.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about everyday things?\n\nPatient: Everyday things… I mean, I suppose everyone worries about bills or appointments or whether they forgot something at the store. I do some of that, sure, but I don’t know if it’s “often” in the way you mean.\n\nSometimes I’ll start thinking about one thing and then it turns into something else entirely, like I’m meant to call the pharmacy and then I notice the counter needs wiping, and then I’m wondering why I came into the kitchen. But worry-worry… I’m not sure that’s the main thing I notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on one task for a long period of time?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I’ll sit down with the senior center newsletter, or sometimes the choir schedule, thinking I’m just going to read through it, and at first I’m fine.\n\nThen a few minutes later I realize my eyes have gone over the same lines two or three times and I don’t actually know what I just read. And then I’m annoyed because it shouldn’t be that hard, you know? Like it’s just a newsletter, not some complicated legal paper.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having a lot of thoughts racing through your mind?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… though it’s less like one big worry and more like my mind keeps getting pulled every which way.\n\nLike if I’m at the kitchen table trying to sort bills or papers, I’ll be doing that, and then a car door shuts outside and suddenly I’m wondering who that is. Or my phone lights up, and I lose where I was. Or I’ll think, “Oh, I need to check the garden,” because the tomatoes were looking a little sad, and then the bills are just sitting there.\n\nSo yes, there’s a lot going on in my head, and it pulls me right off track.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that happen in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I don’t know. I mean, I feel bad if I forget something I said I’d do, like if I meant to call someone back and then the day gets away from me. But guilty about things happening day to day… I’m not sure that’s the right word.\n\nSometimes I’ll think, “Oh, I should’ve handled that better,” but then I also think about ten other things, like whether I left the laundry sitting too long or if I need more milk, and then I’m off thinking about the grocery store. So… I guess I don’t really know if I’d call it guilt.\n\nDoctor: Are these feelings and experiences affecting your daily life or relationships in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, it has. I don’t like admitting that, but it has.\n\nMy family has had to step in with bills and paperwork because I’ll get things mixed up or leave them half-done, and then somebody has to help straighten it out. And at church, or with the garden group, there have been times where I said I’d handle something and then I just… didn’t get it done right, or forgot part of it, and then other people had to redo the plans or cover for me.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly. These are people I care about, and these groups matter to me. I used to feel like I was someone people could count on, and now I get the sense they’re not so sure anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're fidgeting or have an urge to move around when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, especially if I’m supposed to be sitting still for a while.\n\nLike at church meetings, I’ll catch myself tapping my foot, or rubbing the edge of my purse over and over, or shifting around in the chair. And then I notice the ladies beside me looking over, and I think, “Oh, stop doing that,” but a minute later I’m doing something else with my hands.\n\nIt’s funny, because those chairs aren’t very comfortable anyway, and last month they had the room too warm, and I kept thinking someone should crack a window… but yes, I guess the answer is yes. I do have a hard time just sitting still.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things even when there's no obvious reason to worry?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… worry, yes, but I don’t know if it’s for no reason. There’s usually something, even if it’s small, like wondering if I forgot to shut something off or if I said the wrong thing to somebody.\n\nBut then again, sometimes I’ll be sitting there and my mind just grabs onto something. Like the other day I was looking at the mail on the table, and then I remembered I needed stamps, and then I started thinking about the post office parking lot because it’s always so crowded there… so I don’t know if that counts as worrying exactly.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’m just worried all the time for no reason. I’d have to think about it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these feelings of restlessness and difficulty focusing?\n\nPatient: Well… it really started causing real trouble after my husband died. That was about… a year and ten months ago now, I think.\n\nI mean, I suppose I’ve had little things before, everyone does, but since then it’s been much harder to keep myself settled and on track. Time gets funny after someone passes, you know—people say “almost two years” and it feels like yesterday and forever at the same time. But yes, the real problems with it have been since then.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to think about something else?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure exactly what you mean by repeating thoughts. I mean, I’ll get a thought stuck for a bit, like remembering I need to call someone or wondering where I put something, but I don’t know if it’s the kind of thing you’re asking about.\n\nSometimes a song gets in my head—oh, that happens at church too, especially if the choir sings one of the old hymns my mother liked, and then I’ll be humming it later without realizing. But thoughts… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed them repeating in a way that feels strange.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself frequently worrying about ordinary, everyday things, like finances, health, or relationships?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say frequently. I mean, there are ordinary things I think about, of course—everybody has bills come in, or a doctor’s appointment on the calendar, or someone in the family you haven’t heard from in a while.\n\nBut I’m not sure it’s worry like you mean. Sometimes I’ll start thinking about one thing and then it turns into something else, like I’ll remember the electric bill and then think about the kitchen light, and then that reminds me I still haven’t cleaned out that one drawer with all the old batteries in it… and then I’ve lost what I was doing.\n\nSo, everyday things, yes, they come up. But I don’t know that I sit and worry over finances or health or relationships all the time. I’d have to pay more attention to it, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worrying about everyday things is more than what most people would worry about?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to measure that, honestly. I don’t sit there thinking, “Oh, I worry more than other people.” I mean, my neighbor worries about everything—she’ll tell you the whole story about her gutters if you let her—and then my sister hardly seems to worry at all, or at least she doesn’t say it out loud.\n\nFor me, it’s more that things come into my head and I follow them around a bit. Whether that’s more than normal… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worries about everyday things are causing you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Significant distress… I don’t know. I wouldn’t put it that strongly, maybe. I mean, some things bother me, sure, but then I get busy with something else, or I remember I was supposed to take something out of the freezer—actually I did that yesterday and then left it on the counter too long, which was annoying.\n\nBut distress from worrying? I’m not sure. I haven’t really thought of it that way. Things can get in my head, but I don’t know if I’d say it upsets me a lot.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant problems with restlessness or difficulty focusing since childhood, or have these issues developed more recently in your life?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, that goes way back. It’s not just something that started recently. Even in grade school, I remember—well, I don’t remember every bit of it, but my mother kept some of those report cards for years in a box with old birthday cards and things—and the teachers would write that I was bright, but careless.\n\nThey’d say I lost papers, talked when I wasn’t supposed to, needed reminders to stay in my seat or to finish my work. Which, honestly, sounds about right. I’d start something and then get distracted by someone sharpening a pencil or a bird outside the window or whatever. So yes, it’s been there since I was a kid.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these feelings of restlessness and difficulty focusing in most areas of your life, such as at home, with friends, or in other social situations?\n\nPatient: Yes, it’s not just one place. At home it shows up a lot with bills and forms and that kind of paperwork—my family’s had to step in more than once because I either put it somewhere “safe” and then can’t find it, or I don’t finish it.\n\nAnd socially too, I guess. At church, or with the garden group—well, the garden has this whole schedule for who waters what, and there was one year the tomatoes did beautifully, but anyway—people have had to redo plans or cover things I said I’d handle and then dropped. I feel embarrassed about it, honestly. These are people I care about, and it feels like they don’t really think they can count on me anymore.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F90.0", "F90.8"], "note": "Added F90.8 as the patient's symptoms of restlessness, difficulty focusing, and fidgeting are consistent with attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder, and ruled out F41.1 and F48.0 as the patient's symptoms do not strongly indicate anxiety or other neurotic disorders", "is_final": false}

These are people I care about, and it feels like they don’t really think they can count on me anymore.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever been diagnosed with or treated for ADHD?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "patient_turn_index: 18\n(If 1, this is your first reply — return your chief complaint.)\nReturn the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
{"key":"None","name":"None","description":"None","reason":"The question asks about prior ADHD diagnosis or treatment history, which is not covered by any symptom profile item."}

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient experiencing Attention-Deficit/Hyperactivity Disorder (Combined Presentation), in a diagnostic interview with a\npsychiatrist. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you think your worries about everyday things are causing you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Significant distress… I don’t know. I wouldn’t put it that strongly, maybe. I mean, some things bother me, sure, but then I get busy with something else, or I remember I was supposed to take something out of the freezer—actually I did that yesterday and then left it on the counter too long, which was annoying.\n\nBut distress from worrying? I’m not sure. I haven’t really thought of it that way. Things can get in my head, but I don’t know if I’d say it upsets me a lot.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant problems with restlessness or difficulty focusing since childhood, or have these issues developed more recently in your life?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, that goes way back. It’s not just something that started recently. Even in grade school, I remember—well, I don’t remember every bit of it, but my mother kept some of those report cards for years in a box with old birthday cards and things—and the teachers would write that I was bright, but careless.\n\nThey’d say I lost papers, talked when I wasn’t supposed to, needed reminders to stay in my seat or to finish my work. Which, honestly, sounds about right. I’d start something and then get distracted by someone sharpening a pencil or a bird outside the window or whatever. So yes, it’s been there since I was a kid.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these feelings of restlessness and difficulty focusing in most areas of your life, such as at home, with friends, or in other social situations?\n\nPatient: Yes, it’s not just one place. At home it shows up a lot with bills and forms and that kind of paperwork—my family’s had to step in more than once because I either put it somewhere “safe” and then can’t find it, or I don’t finish it.\n\nAnd socially too, I guess. At church, or with the garden group—well, the garden has this whole schedule for who waters what, and there was one year the tomatoes did beautifully, but anyway—people have had to redo plans or cover things I said I’d handle and then dropped. I feel embarrassed about it, honestly. These are people I care about, and it feels like they don’t really think they can count on me anymore.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever been diagnosed with or treated for ADHD?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 66 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure, actually. I don’t think I’ve ever had anyone sit me down and say, “This is what you have,” if that’s what you mean. I mean, doctors have asked me things over the years—usually when I’m there for something completely different, like my blood pressure or that time I had that awful cough that wouldn’t go away—but I don’t remember being formally diagnosed or treated for it.

Unless I’m forgetting something, which is possible. But no, I don’t think so.

---------- turn 18 | doctor:inference | doctor.build_inference_prompt() | model=meta-llama/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct ----------

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess I came in because I just can’t settle down inside myself anymore. It’s like I’m sitting there and my whole body is saying, “Get up, do the next thing,” even when I’m worn out and I know I ought to just sit still. \n\nI’ll be in my chair, and then I’m up looking for something, then I remember the laundry, and—well, not that the laundry matters right now—but it’s that feeling, like I’m jumpy under my skin. I’m just tired of feeling like I can’t rest.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when this restlessness happens?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it anxious, exactly. Maybe sometimes it feels close to that, but it’s more like… I just can’t get comfortable in my own skin.\n\nLike, I’ll sit down and then I’m thinking, did I leave something in the kitchen, and then I remember a drawer I meant to clean out last week—well, that’s not really what you asked. I’m not sure. I notice the restlessness more than I notice feeling scared or worried.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions over and over?\n\nPatient: Repeating certain actions… I’m not sure what you mean exactly. Like checking things, or doing the same thing a set number of times?\n\nI don’t think I have anything like that, not that I’ve really noticed. I mean, I’ll go back into a room and forget why I went there, and then end up doing something else entirely—yesterday I found myself standing by the sink with a towel in my hand, and I had started out looking for my glasses—but I don’t think that’s what you mean by repeating. So… no, not really, unless I’m misunderstanding you.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about everyday things?\n\nPatient: Everyday things… I mean, I suppose everyone worries about bills or appointments or whether they forgot something at the store. I do some of that, sure, but I don’t know if it’s “often” in the way you mean.\n\nSometimes I’ll start thinking about one thing and then it turns into something else entirely, like I’m meant to call the pharmacy and then I notice the counter needs wiping, and then I’m wondering why I came into the kitchen. But worry-worry… I’m not sure that’s the main thing I notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on one task for a long period of time?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I’ll sit down with the senior center newsletter, or sometimes the choir schedule, thinking I’m just going to read through it, and at first I’m fine.\n\nThen a few minutes later I realize my eyes have gone over the same lines two or three times and I don’t actually know what I just read. And then I’m annoyed because it shouldn’t be that hard, you know? Like it’s just a newsletter, not some complicated legal paper.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having a lot of thoughts racing through your mind?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… though it’s less like one big worry and more like my mind keeps getting pulled every which way.\n\nLike if I’m at the kitchen table trying to sort bills or papers, I’ll be doing that, and then a car door shuts outside and suddenly I’m wondering who that is. Or my phone lights up, and I lose where I was. Or I’ll think, “Oh, I need to check the garden,” because the tomatoes were looking a little sad, and then the bills are just sitting there.\n\nSo yes, there’s a lot going on in my head, and it pulls me right off track.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that happen in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I don’t know. I mean, I feel bad if I forget something I said I’d do, like if I meant to call someone back and then the day gets away from me. But guilty about things happening day to day… I’m not sure that’s the right word.\n\nSometimes I’ll think, “Oh, I should’ve handled that better,” but then I also think about ten other things, like whether I left the laundry sitting too long or if I need more milk, and then I’m off thinking about the grocery store. So… I guess I don’t really know if I’d call it guilt.\n\nDoctor: Are these feelings and experiences affecting your daily life or relationships in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, it has. I don’t like admitting that, but it has.\n\nMy family has had to step in with bills and paperwork because I’ll get things mixed up or leave them half-done, and then somebody has to help straighten it out. And at church, or with the garden group, there have been times where I said I’d handle something and then I just… didn’t get it done right, or forgot part of it, and then other people had to redo the plans or cover for me.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly. These are people I care about, and these groups matter to me. I used to feel like I was someone people could count on, and now I get the sense they’re not so sure anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're fidgeting or have an urge to move around when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, especially if I’m supposed to be sitting still for a while.\n\nLike at church meetings, I’ll catch myself tapping my foot, or rubbing the edge of my purse over and over, or shifting around in the chair. And then I notice the ladies beside me looking over, and I think, “Oh, stop doing that,” but a minute later I’m doing something else with my hands.\n\nIt’s funny, because those chairs aren’t very comfortable anyway, and last month they had the room too warm, and I kept thinking someone should crack a window… but yes, I guess the answer is yes. I do have a hard time just sitting still.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things even when there's no obvious reason to worry?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… worry, yes, but I don’t know if it’s for no reason. There’s usually something, even if it’s small, like wondering if I forgot to shut something off or if I said the wrong thing to somebody.\n\nBut then again, sometimes I’ll be sitting there and my mind just grabs onto something. Like the other day I was looking at the mail on the table, and then I remembered I needed stamps, and then I started thinking about the post office parking lot because it’s always so crowded there… so I don’t know if that counts as worrying exactly.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’m just worried all the time for no reason. I’d have to think about it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these feelings of restlessness and difficulty focusing?\n\nPatient: Well… it really started causing real trouble after my husband died. That was about… a year and ten months ago now, I think.\n\nI mean, I suppose I’ve had little things before, everyone does, but since then it’s been much harder to keep myself settled and on track. Time gets funny after someone passes, you know—people say “almost two years” and it feels like yesterday and forever at the same time. But yes, the real problems with it have been since then.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to think about something else?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure exactly what you mean by repeating thoughts. I mean, I’ll get a thought stuck for a bit, like remembering I need to call someone or wondering where I put something, but I don’t know if it’s the kind of thing you’re asking about.\n\nSometimes a song gets in my head—oh, that happens at church too, especially if the choir sings one of the old hymns my mother liked, and then I’ll be humming it later without realizing. But thoughts… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed them repeating in a way that feels strange.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself frequently worrying about ordinary, everyday things, like finances, health, or relationships?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say frequently. I mean, there are ordinary things I think about, of course—everybody has bills come in, or a doctor’s appointment on the calendar, or someone in the family you haven’t heard from in a while.\n\nBut I’m not sure it’s worry like you mean. Sometimes I’ll start thinking about one thing and then it turns into something else, like I’ll remember the electric bill and then think about the kitchen light, and then that reminds me I still haven’t cleaned out that one drawer with all the old batteries in it… and then I’ve lost what I was doing.\n\nSo, everyday things, yes, they come up. But I don’t know that I sit and worry over finances or health or relationships all the time. I’d have to pay more attention to it, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worrying about everyday things is more than what most people would worry about?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to measure that, honestly. I don’t sit there thinking, “Oh, I worry more than other people.” I mean, my neighbor worries about everything—she’ll tell you the whole story about her gutters if you let her—and then my sister hardly seems to worry at all, or at least she doesn’t say it out loud.\n\nFor me, it’s more that things come into my head and I follow them around a bit. Whether that’s more than normal… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worries about everyday things are causing you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Significant distress… I don’t know. I wouldn’t put it that strongly, maybe. I mean, some things bother me, sure, but then I get busy with something else, or I remember I was supposed to take something out of the freezer—actually I did that yesterday and then left it on the counter too long, which was annoying.\n\nBut distress from worrying? I’m not sure. I haven’t really thought of it that way. Things can get in my head, but I don’t know if I’d say it upsets me a lot.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant problems with restlessness or difficulty focusing since childhood, or have these issues developed more recently in your life?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, that goes way back. It’s not just something that started recently. Even in grade school, I remember—well, I don’t remember every bit of it, but my mother kept some of those report cards for years in a box with old birthday cards and things—and the teachers would write that I was bright, but careless.\n\nThey’d say I lost papers, talked when I wasn’t supposed to, needed reminders to stay in my seat or to finish my work. Which, honestly, sounds about right. I’d start something and then get distracted by someone sharpening a pencil or a bird outside the window or whatever. So yes, it’s been there since I was a kid.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these feelings of restlessness and difficulty focusing in most areas of your life, such as at home, with friends, or in other social situations?\n\nPatient: Yes, it’s not just one place. At home it shows up a lot with bills and forms and that kind of paperwork—my family’s had to step in more than once because I either put it somewhere “safe” and then can’t find it, or I don’t finish it.\n\nAnd socially too, I guess. At church, or with the garden group—well, the garden has this whole schedule for who waters what, and there was one year the tomatoes did beautifully, but anyway—people have had to redo plans or cover things I said I’d handle and then dropped. I feel embarrassed about it, honestly. These are people I care about, and it feels like they don’t really think they can count on me anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with or treated for ADHD?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, actually. I don’t think I’ve ever had anyone sit me down and say, “This is what you have,” if that’s what you mean. I mean, doctors have asked me things over the years—usually when I’m there for something completely different, like my blood pressure or that time I had that awful cough that wouldn’t go away—but I don’t remember being formally diagnosed or treated for it.\n\nUnless I’m forgetting something, which is possible. But no, I don’t think so.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

I notice the restlessness more than I notice feeling scared or worried.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions over and over?\n\nPatient: Repeating certain actions… I’m not sure what you mean exactly. Like checking things, or doing the same thing a set number of times?\n\nI don’t think I have anything like that, not that I’ve really noticed. I mean, I’ll go back into a room and forget why I went there, and then end up doing something else entirely—yesterday I found myself standing by the sink with a towel in my hand, and I had started out looking for my glasses—but I don’t think that’s what you mean by repeating. So… no, not really, unless I’m misunderstanding you.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about everyday things?\n\nPatient: Everyday things… I mean, I suppose everyone worries about bills or appointments or whether they forgot something at the store. I do some of that, sure, but I don’t know if it’s “often” in the way you mean.\n\nSometimes I’ll start thinking about one thing and then it turns into something else entirely, like I’m meant to call the pharmacy and then I notice the counter needs wiping, and then I’m wondering why I came into the kitchen. But worry-worry… I’m not sure that’s the main thing I notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on one task for a long period of time?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I’ll sit down with the senior center newsletter, or sometimes the choir schedule, thinking I’m just going to read through it, and at first I’m fine.\n\nThen a few minutes later I realize my eyes have gone over the same lines two or three times and I don’t actually know what I just read. And then I’m annoyed because it shouldn’t be that hard, you know? Like it’s just a newsletter, not some complicated legal paper.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having a lot of thoughts racing through your mind?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… though it’s less like one big worry and more like my mind keeps getting pulled every which way.\n\nLike if I’m at the kitchen table trying to sort bills or papers, I’ll be doing that, and then a car door shuts outside and suddenly I’m wondering who that is. Or my phone lights up, and I lose where I was. Or I’ll think, “Oh, I need to check the garden,” because the tomatoes were looking a little sad, and then the bills are just sitting there.\n\nSo yes, there’s a lot going on in my head, and it pulls me right off track.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that happen in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I don’t know. I mean, I feel bad if I forget something I said I’d do, like if I meant to call someone back and then the day gets away from me. But guilty about things happening day to day… I’m not sure that’s the right word.\n\nSometimes I’ll think, “Oh, I should’ve handled that better,” but then I also think about ten other things, like whether I left the laundry sitting too long or if I need more milk, and then I’m off thinking about the grocery store. So… I guess I don’t really know if I’d call it guilt.\n\nDoctor: Are these feelings and experiences affecting your daily life or relationships in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, it has. I don’t like admitting that, but it has.\n\nMy family has had to step in with bills and paperwork because I’ll get things mixed up or leave them half-done, and then somebody has to help straighten it out. And at church, or with the garden group, there have been times where I said I’d handle something and then I just… didn’t get it done right, or forgot part of it, and then other people had to redo the plans or cover for me.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly. These are people I care about, and these groups matter to me. I used to feel like I was someone people could count on, and now I get the sense they’re not so sure anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're fidgeting or have an urge to move around when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, especially if I’m supposed to be sitting still for a while.\n\nLike at church meetings, I’ll catch myself tapping my foot, or rubbing the edge of my purse over and over, or shifting around in the chair. And then I notice the ladies beside me looking over, and I think, “Oh, stop doing that,” but a minute later I’m doing something else with my hands.\n\nIt’s funny, because those chairs aren’t very comfortable anyway, and last month they had the room too warm, and I kept thinking someone should crack a window… but yes, I guess the answer is yes. I do have a hard time just sitting still.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things even when there's no obvious reason to worry?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… worry, yes, but I don’t know if it’s for no reason. There’s usually something, even if it’s small, like wondering if I forgot to shut something off or if I said the wrong thing to somebody.\n\nBut then again, sometimes I’ll be sitting there and my mind just grabs onto something. Like the other day I was looking at the mail on the table, and then I remembered I needed stamps, and then I started thinking about the post office parking lot because it’s always so crowded there… so I don’t know if that counts as worrying exactly.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’m just worried all the time for no reason. I’d have to think about it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these feelings of restlessness and difficulty focusing?\n\nPatient: Well… it really started causing real trouble after my husband died. That was about… a year and ten months ago now, I think.\n\nI mean, I suppose I’ve had little things before, everyone does, but since then it’s been much harder to keep myself settled and on track. Time gets funny after someone passes, you know—people say “almost two years” and it feels like yesterday and forever at the same time. But yes, the real problems with it have been since then.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to think about something else?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure exactly what you mean by repeating thoughts. I mean, I’ll get a thought stuck for a bit, like remembering I need to call someone or wondering where I put something, but I don’t know if it’s the kind of thing you’re asking about.\n\nSometimes a song gets in my head—oh, that happens at church too, especially if the choir sings one of the old hymns my mother liked, and then I’ll be humming it later without realizing. But thoughts… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed them repeating in a way that feels strange.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself frequently worrying about ordinary, everyday things, like finances, health, or relationships?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say frequently. I mean, there are ordinary things I think about, of course—everybody has bills come in, or a doctor’s appointment on the calendar, or someone in the family you haven’t heard from in a while.\n\nBut I’m not sure it’s worry like you mean. Sometimes I’ll start thinking about one thing and then it turns into something else, like I’ll remember the electric bill and then think about the kitchen light, and then that reminds me I still haven’t cleaned out that one drawer with all the old batteries in it… and then I’ve lost what I was doing.\n\nSo, everyday things, yes, they come up. But I don’t know that I sit and worry over finances or health or relationships all the time. I’d have to pay more attention to it, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worrying about everyday things is more than what most people would worry about?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to measure that, honestly. I don’t sit there thinking, “Oh, I worry more than other people.” I mean, my neighbor worries about everything—she’ll tell you the whole story about her gutters if you let her—and then my sister hardly seems to worry at all, or at least she doesn’t say it out loud.\n\nFor me, it’s more that things come into my head and I follow them around a bit. Whether that’s more than normal… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worries about everyday things are causing you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Significant distress… I don’t know. I wouldn’t put it that strongly, maybe. I mean, some things bother me, sure, but then I get busy with something else, or I remember I was supposed to take something out of the freezer—actually I did that yesterday and then left it on the counter too long, which was annoying.\n\nBut distress from worrying? I’m not sure. I haven’t really thought of it that way. Things can get in my head, but I don’t know if I’d say it upsets me a lot.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant problems with restlessness or difficulty focusing since childhood, or have these issues developed more recently in your life?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, that goes way back. It’s not just something that started recently. Even in grade school, I remember—well, I don’t remember every bit of it, but my mother kept some of those report cards for years in a box with old birthday cards and things—and the teachers would write that I was bright, but careless.\n\nThey’d say I lost papers, talked when I wasn’t supposed to, needed reminders to stay in my seat or to finish my work. Which, honestly, sounds about right. I’d start something and then get distracted by someone sharpening a pencil or a bird outside the window or whatever. So yes, it’s been there since I was a kid.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these feelings of restlessness and difficulty focusing in most areas of your life, such as at home, with friends, or in other social situations?\n\nPatient: Yes, it’s not just one place. At home it shows up a lot with bills and forms and that kind of paperwork—my family’s had to step in more than once because I either put it somewhere “safe” and then can’t find it, or I don’t finish it.\n\nAnd socially too, I guess. At church, or with the garden group—well, the garden has this whole schedule for who waters what, and there was one year the tomatoes did beautifully, but anyway—people have had to redo plans or cover things I said I’d handle and then dropped. I feel embarrassed about it, honestly. These are people I care about, and it feels like they don’t really think they can count on me anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with or treated for ADHD?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, actually. I don’t think I’ve ever had anyone sit me down and say, “This is what you have,” if that’s what you mean. I mean, doctors have asked me things over the years—usually when I’m there for something completely different, like my blood pressure or that time I had that awful cough that wouldn’t go away—but I don’t remember being formally diagnosed or treated for it.\n\nUnless I’m forgetting something, which is possible. But no, I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had difficulty focusing and felt restless, even as a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, yes, I think I’ve always been that way. Even back in grade school, my teachers would write things like, “bright but careless,” which my mother used to bring up—she kept those report cards, you know, in this old folder with the school pictures.\n\nThey’d say I was always losing papers, talking when I shouldn’t, and needing reminders to stay in my seat or finish my work. So it wasn’t something that just came on later. It was there when I was little too.\n\nDoctor: Have you consistently experienced difficulties with focus and restlessness across most areas of your life for as long as you can remember?\n\nPatient: Yes, for as long as I can remember, really. I mean, even back in grade school my teachers would write things like I was “bright but careless”—my mother used to get so frustrated seeing that. They’d say I was always losing papers, talking out of turn, needing reminders to stay in my seat or finish my work.\n\nIt’s funny, I can still picture those report cards, though I couldn’t tell you where half my important papers are now. But yes, it’s been there since I was little.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
